#2784. Classic Cousins, Dominant Defense Lead Way as Vikings Thrash Packers

For the first eight games of their 2026 season, the Minnesota Vikings' offense struggled to put points on the board while their defense made life miserable for offenses around the NFL. On Sunday afternoon at Lambeau Field, it appears that the tide might be turning for the Minnesota offense, thanks to the addition of one man.

Kirk Cousins threw a pair of touchdown passes and, more importantly, allowed the Minnesota defense to actually get some rest after two straight weeks of getting overworked. The defense responded by sacking Jordan Love eight times and shutting down an offense that had scored 45 points the previous week in a thoroughly dominant 30-10 win over the Green Bay Packers in Week 10 action at Lambeau Field.

The Packers won the opening coin toss and deferred to the second half, putting Kirk Cousins and the Minnesota offense on the field first. The Vikings went three-and-out, as Cousins' first pass was too high for Justin Jefferson and his second, a screen to Myles Price, was dropped for a loss of yards. Brett Thorson blasted a 64-yard punt, and the Packers started their first possession inside their own 10-yard line.

On the Packers' second play from scrimmage, Andrew Van Ginkel made his presence felt, as Jordan Love completed a pass to tight end Mark Redman, and Van Ginkel stripped it away, recovering the fumble at the Green Bay 10 for a turnover.

The Vikings' offense capitalized on the turnover with. . .a false start and a sack of Cousins that moved them back 14 yards in two plays. After an Aaron Jones run on third down, the Vikings called on Will Reichard, who connected on a 34-yard field goal to give the Vikings a 3-0 lead just under five minutes into the contest.

Green Bay got some help on the ensuing kickoff, as Jay Ward committed a 15-yard penalty to put the Packers near midfield to start their next possession. The Packers moved the ball into Minnesota territory inside the 30-yard line, but the drive came to a screeching halt when Dallas Turner dropped Love for a sack. The Packers brought on Trey Smack to attempt a 53-yard field goal, but his attempt sailed wide to the right to give Minnesota the ball back near midfield, still leading 3-0.

Minnesota started their next drive with a first down into Green Bay territory, as Cousins found Gavin Bartholomew to move the chains. The Vikings then got a look at a familiar connection, as Cousins found Justin Jefferson for a 13-yard gain to convert on third down and keep the drive moving. The Vikings were then called for a face mask penalty to push themselves back to the edge of field goal range. . .but Cousins decided that the Vikings didn't need a field goal, as he hit Myles Price for a 38-yard touchdown that saw Price bounce off of a Green Bay defender and make his way into the end zone.

The first receiving touchdown of Price's NFL career increased the Minnesota lead to 10-0 with just over two minutes left in the first quarter. And in the ninth game of the season, that was the Vikings' first offensive touchdown in the first quarter of any game this season.

Green Bay got off to a quick start on their next drive, as Love found Marshawn Lloyd on a quick pass for a big gain to near midfield, and Lloyd then took a handoff into Minnesota territory for another first down. The Vikings then got called for a couple of 15-yard penalties, one on Eric Wilson for roughing the passer and another on Dallas Turner for. . .something. . .to help move the Packers into the red zone. But when it looked like the Packers might get on the scoreboard, Harrison Smith decided to put an end to all that.

The Hitman picked off Jordan Love in the end zone to end the drive and bring the ball out to the 20-yard line for the Vikings' next possession. Unfortunately, the Vikings' offense couldn't do anything with it, as a loss on first down stalled the drive early as they went three-and-out again. Thorson's punt put the Green Bay offense at its own 25 to start their next possession.

The Vikings' defense went to work again, as they registered back-to-back sacks of Love to force a three-and-out, one from Levi Drake Rodriguez and one from Eric Wilson. Green Bay's punt gave the Vikings good field position at their own 45-yard line to start their next possession. The Vikings marched the ball down the field with a nice mix of passes before a run by Jordan Mason put the ball into the red zone.

The Vikings then dodged a bullet when it looked like Cousins had thrown an interception, but it was ruled by replay that his knee was down before he threw the ball, allowing the Vikings to keep possession. Cousins then hit Tai Felton on a big third-down throw to make it first-and-goal. On the next play, the former Packer, Aaron Jones, took a pitch and went around the left side for a 2-yard score to extend the Minnesota lead to 17-0 with just over six minutes left in the first half.

Green Bay's offense went three-and-out on its next possession after a third-down catch by Matthew Golden that had been ruled a first-down completion was ruled incomplete after Kevin O'Connell challenged the call, as replay showed that the ball hit the ground before Golden controlled it. Minnesota took over again and pushed into Green Bay territory before the two-minute warning, but on the first snap after the two-minute timeout, Cousins' pass for Tai Felton was overthrown and intercepted by Xavier McKinney for a turnover.

The Packers took the ball after the interception and got the ball close to midfield, but then the Vikings' defense turned things up again and collected two more sacks of Love: one that was split between Van Ginkel and Kyle Van Noy, and one that Van Noy got all by himself. That concluded a five-sack first half for the Vikings, and they went into the locker room with a 17-0 lead.

Green Bay got the ball to start the second half, and pushed their first possession to near midfield. However, they turned the ball over again when a shotgun snap flew right past Jordan Love for a fumble that Dallas Turner recovered at the Green Bay 27-yard line for the third turnover of the game for Green Bay, setting the Vikings up nicely.

The Vikings got the ball into a goal-to-go situation, but the offense stalled, and they had to settle for another field goal from Reichard, this one a 32-yarder to make it 20-0 with ten minutes left in the third quarter.

The Packers got the ball back and once again moved into Minnesota territory, but the sack parade continued again, with Jalen Redmond and Elijah Williams taking Love down to add to the total. The Packers faced a 4th-and-11 from the Minnesota 44-yard line, and they converted on a pass from Love to Matthew Golden to stay on the field. On the next play, Green Bay finally got on the scoreboard, as Love found J. Michael Sturdivant for a 32-yard score to make it 20-7 with just over five minutes remaining in the third quarter of play.

Minnesota went three-and-out on its next possession, putting Green Bay at their 14-yard line to start their next drive. Love and company went back to work and started to heat up, working the middle of the field with passes to Christian Watson and Tucker Kraft to push the ball towards the red zone. But their drive stalled out, and they settled for a 40-yard field goal attempt from Smack, who put it between the uprights to make the score 20-10 with just 19 seconds left in the third quarter.

The Vikings took over on offense and put together one of their best offensive drives of the season, mixing runs and passes while getting a few key third-down conversions, pushing the ball into the red zone and eating six and a half minutes off the clock. The drive ended with the first NFL touchdown in the career of tight end Gavin Bartholomew, who took a pass from Cousins and turned it into a 20-yard score to put the Vikings up 27-10 with eight and a half minutes remaining in regulation.

On the ensuing kickoff, the Packers gave up turnover number four, as Jakobe Thomas. . .who was active for the first time in his NFL career today after giving his #8 to Kirk Cousins. . .forced a fumble on the return from Sturdivant, and the ball was recovered by Jake Golday at the Green Bay 35-yard line.

Minnesota got the ball down to the 2-yard line, but wound up giving Will Reichard another field goal attempt, a 20-yarder that split the uprights to extend the Vikings' lead to 30-10 with just under four minutes remaining in regulation.

The Packers officially raised the white flag on their next drive, taking Love out of the game and putting Tyrod Taylor into the game at quarterback. For the afternoon, Love was officially sacked eight times by Minnesota defenders, the most by a Vikings team ever in a game at Lambeau Field and two off of the franchise record for the most sacks in a game.

Cousins was solid, if not spectacular, which is really all the Vikings need at this point. He completed 21-of-31 passes for 207 yards, a pair of touchdown passes, and an interception. More importantly, the Vikings held on to the ball for over thirty-five minutes after two weeks where their opponents were able to keep the Minnesota defense on the field for extended periods of time and wear them down

The victory snapped Minnesota's two-game losing streak and put them into a three-way tie atop the NFC North with the Chicago Bears, who had Week 10 off, and the Detroit Lions, who thumped the New England Patriots in Munich, Germany, earlier in the day. All three teams have 7-2 records. It won't get any easier for the Vikings next week, as they'll make a trip to Mexico City to face another of the NFL's best teams, the San Francisco 49ers, on Sunday Night Football.

The Vikings sweep the 2026 Border Battle from the Green Bay Packers, taking it home with a thoroughly convincing 30-10 win at Lambeau Field in Week 10. Thank you to everybody who got their coverage of this week's game right here at The Daily Norseman!

#2783. 'We slept here': Flight delays linger but schedule is back on track for Monday

U.S. flights are set to run close to normal on Monday as airlines scramble to get back on track after the Federal Aviation Administration grounded departures nationwide for more than four hours on Friday.

Disruptions lingered on Sunday with thousands more flights canceled and delayed.

Close to 2,000 U.S. flights were canceled as of 4:45 p.m. ET and more than 7,500 were delayed, according to flight-tracking site FlightAware, down from the 6,697 cancellations and 9,000 delays on Saturday, and Friday's 9,201 cancellations and more than 7,400 delays.

American Airlines had the most cancellations on Sunday at 805 flights, about 13% of its schedule, followed by Delta Air Lines with 360 cancellations, or 7% of its schedule, according to aviation data firm Cirium. The airlines' home hubs of Dallas-Fort Worth International Airport and Atlanta Hartsfield-Jackson International Airport, respectively, saw some of the most severe delays in the U.S. Meanwhile, JetBlue Airways canceled close to a third of its flights.

"We slept here," said Beth Martin, who was trying to travel home to Birmingham, Alabama, from New York's LaGuardia Airport on Sunday at the end of her daughter's birthday trip. They were booked for Saturday night, and when delay announcements started rolling in from Delta Air Lines, they decided to make good use of the extra time: after strolling through Central Park, they went window shopping on Fifth Avenue.

But when the flight was canceled, the standby list for a backup flight looked grim, and their original Midtown hotel was charging about $700 for an additional night, the two slept at LaGuardia on Saturday. She said they found wheelchairs to sleep in outside security.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy on Saturday tied the issue to a router and work to update from copper wires, a system overseen by L3Harris Technologies, to a fiber-optic system. FAA administrator Bryan Bedford said the upgrades were part of "routine" maintenance to switch over to fiber optic.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," L3Harris said in a statement Saturday afternoon.

Airlines waived change fees and fare differences and offered pilots and flight attendants extra pay to ease the delays.

Customers told CNBC they were booked on several flights before getting on board.

Justine Fritz, who works in sales for a consumer goods company, was in New York for a business event on Friday. She tried five different flights before getting on a flight to Houston, then flew four hours out of her way to get back to Minneapolis after her Saturday flight was canceled. Fritz said she had so many boarding passes that TSA wouldn't let her through security at first.

"It's been bananas," she told CNBC at the gate. Fritz said she made the best of it by shopping and getting a haircut, but she's still disappointed.

"I'm missing the first hour of the Vikings game," she said.

Martin said her trip home with her daughter will take even longer. Delta booked them to Memphis, and her husband will drive the four hours from Birmingham to pick them up.

Shortly after the groundstop, the longest since 9/11, Duffy pushed Congress for as much as $50 billion for upgrades to the aging U.S. air traffic control system. A day later, Democratic lawmakers pushed back, citing unspent money from existing funding, which includes Congress's approval last year of $12.5 billion for airport and air traffic control improvements over five years.

"Our committee, on behalf of the flying public, will hold the administration accountable for what happened to make sure this chaos does not happen again," said Reps. Rick Larsen (D.-Wash.), ranking member of the House Committee on Transportation and Infrastructure, and Rep. Andre Carson (D.-Ind.), ranking member of the aviation subcommittee, said in a joint statement.

#2782. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2780. Aidan Hutchinson out of game after suffering knee injury celebrating a sack

Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson has been ruled out of the game after injuring his knee celebrating a sack.

Hutchinson sacked Drake Maye on third-and-goal to end the Patriots’ first offensive possession. Hutchinson then got up to celebrate, jumped into the air pumping his fist, and landed awkwardly. His right knee appeared to buckle.

Although Hutchinson was able to limp off the field under his own power, it looked like a potentially serious injury for Hutchinson, a Defensive Player of the Year candidate.

Maye was also injured when Hutchinson sacked him: Maye hurt his ankle and was ruled out of the game.

Injuries have affected both teams, but the Lions are in better shape with a 28-3 lead in the fourth quarter.

#2778. Anthropic’s IPO lands as the embattled AI trade looks for a much-needed bright spot

Between rising interest rates, a nationwide AI backlash, a major political shift in Washington, D.C., and the sudden ouster of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the tech industry is under mounting strain.

Now comes the most important tech IPO in years, and a defining event for the burgeoning but still young artificial intelligence market.

With Anthropic expected to debut on the Nasdaq on Thursday, the stakes are sky-high for almost every major tech company. Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company are collectively investing $26 billion at the IPO price, which is currently expected at $70 to $78 a share. Some of those companies are existing investors, as is early backer Amazon, whose stake could be in the hundreds of billions of dollars.

In addition to all the cash on the line, tech giants also need Anthropic's spending for many years to come. The Claude creator and its top rival, OpenAI, are driving an outsized amount of the AI buildout, and are burning historic amounts of money in the process.

Anthropic has outstanding spending commitments of over $900 billion, according to its latest prospectus. It's on the hook to spend at least $110 billion with Amazon over the course of a decade, and another $400 billion with other companies for cloud capacity, according to filings and partner disclosures. The lab also made $300 billion in commitments with data center and chip partners. Anthropic intends to lay out $125 billion to $150 billion for AI chips from Google, though delivery won't start until 2028.

"The numbers are just staggering," Joe Fath, a partner at venture firm Eclipse and former T. Rowe Price portfolio manager, said in an interview. "It's hard to get your head wrapped around it."

Cloud partners Amazon, Google and Microsoft account for 31% of contractual obligations, Anthropic said in its prospectus. Last year, Amazon built an Indiana data center just for Anthropic, using custom chips.

By going public now, Anthropic can raise a large portion of the money it needs to pay for the technology and infrastructure required for building and hosting its frontier models and expanding portfolio of services. The company recorded a net loss of $104 billion through the first nine months of 2026. The IPO could yield over $90 billion in proceeds, on top of Anthropic's $132 billion in existing cash, including short-term investments.

The length of Anthropic's contracts is also unique. Typically such contracts last five years or less, while Anthropic's agreement with Amazon extends for twice that amount of time.

"Realistically, anything past three to four years, you're basically throwing darts in the dark," said Corey Quinn, chief cloud economist at spend management software vendor Duckbill. "It is almost a certainty that it will be renegotiated long before the culmination of that contract."

Meanwhile, a data center for Anthropic that starts leasing in 2027 has a 20-year term. For both space and computing, Anthropic is splurging now so it doesn't end up short on capacity long into the future.

"In the past, we have significantly underestimated our compute requirements, which left us capacity constrained and impacted both product development and deployment," Anthropic said in the prospectus.

Anthropic and Amazon didn't provide a comment.

It's a tough time for Anthropic to hit the Nasdaq, with the offering coming as tech investors are desperate for some good news. The Nasdaq is down to start November after dropping 5.3% in October, its worst month since March 2025.

Rising interest rates pushed investors out of debt-heavy neoclouds, which have become a critical piece of the infrastructure buildout. And in the midterm elections earlier this month, Democrats won sweeping victories in both houses of Congress and in key gubernatorial races, while dozens of ballot measures were passed that will potentially limit new data center development across the country.

Then came the Nvidia crisis. Shares of the chipmaker at the center of the AI boom tumbled 13% this week to their lowest since July 2025, after Huang's multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, led to his ouster early Thursday. Huang was replaced on an interim basis with CFO Colette Cress, who faces a major test on Tuesday, two days before the Anthropic debut, as Nvidia is set to report quarterly results.

The ongoing Nvidia fallout and the Anthropic IPO create quite the split-screen moment for tech's heavyweights, which are closely tied to both companies.

Google will be paying particularly close attention.

The company is set to own about 13% of Anthropic after the IPO, and is backstopping the lab's $19 billion in lease commitments to Terawulf, which will supply data center space in Kentucky. If Anthropic goes insolvent, Google will step in and pay the remaining rent or a termination fee, Terawulf said.

"Everybody knows that Anthropic is going to be the future of Google," said Subhadeep Chatterjee, a former Google cloud partnerships leader who now advises companies.

One major impetus for Anthropic to enter costly and long-term guarantees is that OpenAI is being equally aggressive, and capacity is limited, particularly for graphics processing units. Going long can mean locking in rates as the industry confronts questions about whether data center development will slow.

"You will need to be in a rush to get infrastructure, and if you are not going to be there, you will be out of business," said Eli Mansoor, CEO of OskaQ Consulting, which advises companies on cloud negotiations. "It used to be that prices always go down, but this is changing, especially in the GPU world."

Big financial firms are lining up to help Anthropic get what it wants. Bank of America, Blackstone, Citigroup and Morgan Stanley have offered $63 billion to buy racks full of Broadcom AI chips that Anthropic will rent. Broadcom has provided a backstop with a maximum liability of $43 billion.

That's a lot of silicon and a massive bet for a five-year-old company that's never lived through a technology downturn.

"It's not a risk-free prospect, but I would call it smart investing," said David Tollen, founder of Sycamore Legal, where he specializes in IT contracts. "They're investing, in a sense, in themselves. They're putting money into something that if it succeeds, they get a piece of the action."

#2777. Jahmyr Gibbs totals 171 yards in first half as Lions roll over Patriots

Lions running back Jahmyr Gibbs is making a mockery of the Patriots’ defense today in Munich, Germany.

Gibbs has 171 yards from scrimmage and a touchdown as the Lions have cruised to a 21-3 lead late in the second quarter. Gibbs has 13 carries for 93 yards and a touchdown, plus another four catches for 78 receiving yards. The Patriots’ defense has had no answer for him.

It’s the second consecutive ugly outing for New England’s defense, which was shredded by the Packers’ passing game last week.

The Patriots’ offense is also struggling, but that appears to be mostly due to the injury suffered by quarterback Drake Maye on New England’s opening possession. The Patriots had been marching down the field until Maye was sacked by Aidan Hutchinson on third-and-goal, and Maye was knocked out of the game with an ankle injury. After the field goal immediately following that sack, the Patriots haven’t scored.

It’s hard to imagine the Patriots mounting a second-half comeback with Tommy DeVito at the helm, but that’s what they’ll have to do if they’re going to avoid heading home from Germany on a two-game losing streak.

#2776. Antetokounmpo scores 34, Heat block 14 shots and roll past Pistons 137-117 for 4th straight win

MIAMI — Giannis Antetokounmpo had 34 points, 14 rebounds and eight assists, some of the spectacular variety, and the Miami Heat beat the Detroit Pistons 137-117 on Saturday night for their fourth straight win.

Klay Thompson scored 26 points in his return from a sprained ankle and Bam Adebayo added 20 for the Heat. Davion Mitchell had 15 points and nine rebounds for Miami.

Cade Cunningham had 33 points and eight assists for Detroit, which got 20 points and seven assists from Kevin Huerter.

Miami had nine players record at least one block and finished with 14 as a team — one shy of the franchise record.

The NBA record for players on the same team having at least one block in a game is 10, done twice — both by Golden State, first in 1991 and then again in 2016. The Heat team record for players with at least one block was eight, done on five occasions.

Antetokounmpo finished a weekend where he scored 71 points in a two-day span, after finishing with 37 in Miami's win over Cleveland on Friday night. He also had a through-the-legs assist to Thompson for a 3-pointer in the opening quarter.

It was one of six 3s on the night for Thompson, who is now 26 away from tying former Heat guard Ray Allen (2,973) for the third-most 3-pointers in NBA history.

Miami won for the seventh time in its last nine games and became the fourth team in the last 25 seasons to start 0-4 but rally to a winning record at the 13-game mark. Sacramento (7-6) did it in 2022-23, Oklahoma City (8-5) did it in 2018-19 and Dallas (9-4) did it in 2006-07.

It also matched the best nine-game stretch for the Heat since an 8-1 burst in November 2023.

Pistons: Visit San Antonio on Tuesday night.

#2775. NFC Playoff Picture: Chicago Bears fall to 6th with the Vikings win

The Chicago Bears are on their bye this weekend, but after the Detroit Lions beat the New England Patriots in Germany, 35 to 10, they've leapfrogged the Bears into first place in the NFC North. That also dropped the Bears down to the fifth seed in the conference.

The Packers and Vikings are playing right now, and a Minnesota win would move them up to the fifth seed, so adjust your rooting interests accordingly.

UPDATE: The Vikings beat the Packers 30 to 10, so they jumped ahead of the Bears for the fifth seed, dropping the Bears down to sixth. While three NFC North teams are 7-2, the Vikings have the tiebreaker over the Bears, and the Lions have the tiebreaker over Minnesota.

We'll update these standings later today, but for now here's hw the NFC shapes up.

#2774. Bessent calls for ‘dramatic shakeup’ at Nvidia, urges outside CEO

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent on Sunday called for a "dramatic shakeup" at Nvidia after CEO Jensen Huang's resignation, questioning whether the chipmaker pursued illicit sales of its advanced artificial intelligence chips to boost earnings.

Bessent said Nvidia's board should look outside the company for its next chief executive and suggested other executives should face scrutiny over the chip giant's compliance with U.S. export controls.

"I want to keep coming back to what was the culture at Nvidia, and were they pressing these seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings?" Bessent said in an interview on Fox News.

The remarks come amid a growing feud between Bessent and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick over the suspected diversion of advanced AI chips to China, even as the Treasury secretary continues to defend President Donald Trump's handling of the matter.

Both Bessent and Lutnick are expected to accompany Trump to China this week, Semafor reported Sunday, putting the two Cabinet secretaries alongside the president as he prepares to meet with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin on the sidelines of the APEC summit.

A White House official told Reuters on Thursday that the president maintains "complete confidence" in Lutnick, despite mounting scrutiny of the secretary’s handling of the Nvidia matter.

Huang resigned Thursday following revelations about his relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed, whom U.S. intelligence officials suspect of spying for China. Megaspeed is suspected of diverting Nvidia chips to China in violation of U.S. export controls.

Nvidia named Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress interim CEO.

Bessent suggested Sunday that Nvidia's problems extended well beyond Huang.

"If this were a bank and they hadn't complied like this, the OCC would be in there right now, and a lot of the executives would be removed," Bessent said, referring to the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

Bessent also questioned Nvidia's financial disclosures, pointing to a surge in reported Singapore revenue as its China sales fell.

Nvidia stopped separately reporting Singapore revenue in November 2025, saying a new methodology based on customers' headquarters rather than billing addresses better reflected the geographic distribution of its sales.

Bessent's remarks came two days after he told CNBC's "Squawk Box" that several federal agencies had supported adding Megaspeed to the government's export blacklist for months.

"But Commerce has the pen," Bessent said.

The public rebuke followed reporting by Axios that Bessent confronted Lutnick in the White House Situation Room over Commerce's failure to act against Megaspeed. Commerce denied that the confrontation took place.

Lutnick is also facing scrutiny over his family's financial ties to Nvidia. CNBC reported Friday that Cantor Fitzgerald, the Wall Street firm formerly run by Lutnick and now led by his sons, has helped arrange billions of dollars in financing for Nvidia's business partners.

Cantor said Nvidia share holdings fell nearly 50% over six months as national security concerns surrounding Huang escalated. The positions were associated with client swap transactions, not proprietary investments, the investment bank said.

There is no evidence Lutnick shared sensitive information with his sons or that Cantor traded on such information.

Bessent on Sunday said Trump had drawn a firm line against sales of chips more advanced than Nvidia's H200 to China. Trump himself authorized H200 sales to approved Chinese customers in December —following months of lobbying by Huang — in an arrangement under which the U.S. government would receive a 25% cut.

Bessent's latest criticism comes two days before Nvidia is scheduled to report its first quarterly earnings since Huang's departure.

#2773. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2771. Diving deeper into Cavs 127-117 win over Mavs: Tyrese Proctor leads bench crew to unexpected victory

CLEVELAND — The Cleveland Cavaliers all but punted this game after not getting back from Miami until late this morning. They rested four of the five starters from last night's game (Donovan Mitchell, James Harden, Jarrett Allen, Peyton Watson) rather than risk injury on the second night of a back-to-back. Yet sometimes, the receiving team muffs the punt.

The Dallas Mavericks — who weren't resting their starters — took their eye off the ball. Cleveland took advantage, led by a triple-double from Tyrese Proctor to ruin Kyrie Irving's homecoming and defeat Dallas 127-117.

24 points on 10-of-19 shooting, 11 rebounds, 10 assists, 1 turnover

Proctor has been a revelation over the last week. He backed up an impressive performance in Miami — albeit in a supporting role — with a dominant on-ball showing against the Mavericks.

This was a different outing than what we've seen from Proctor in the past. Instead of being mostly an off-ball shooter, he thrived at creating his own shots. Only one of his 10 field goals was assisted. This included going 5-for-8 on off-the-dribble jumpers.

Scoring wasn't the only thing Proctor provided. He was the primary initiator of the offense, as evidenced by his 10 assists. Some of these led to direct baskets at the rim for Thomas Bryant (19 points and 11 rebounds) out of the pick-and-roll. Overall, six of his assists went to baskets at the rim. He also sprayed the ball to outside shooters and swung the ball on the perimeter, leading to three assists on triples.

Proctor also helped as a defender and rebounder. Kenny Atkinson often talks about how the guards need to be adequate rebounders as well. Proctor was that in a complete all-around performance.

This season, Proctor has been asked to fit into a very specific role. He's done that well, but nights like this show that he's capable of more.

34 points on 11-of-25 shooting, 6 rebounds, 5 assists, 3 steals

Irving's 20 second-quarter points got the Mavs back into this game. He still can be a one-man offense for extended stretches and completely take over games. This was another impressive scoring performance from someone who's had more than his fair share in this building.

22 points on 9-of-12 shooting, 7 rebounds, 4 assists

Hezonja made a strong argument for why he deserves more minutes than he's been getting in recent games. He forced mismatches offensively, and then read how the defense was going to react and consistently made the right play out of it. Hozonja processes the game at such a high level. This elevates the basketball IQ for units he's on the court with.

Back-to-backs are difficult, especially when travel plans are thrown off as drastically as they were around the country yesterday. It makes sense to err on the side of caution in a situation like this. What's the point of risking an injury for a game that will matter very little in the spring? At the same time, saying that the game should be canceled because of an inconvenience like this is a bad look.

On top of that, the Cavs played some of their most inspiring basketball of the early season on Saturday night without six of the top eight players in their rotation. That is good for the guys who played, but a clear indictment on those who didn't.

Atkinson was asked about rebounding before the game since it's been a consistent issue with the starting group. He gave a long-winded answer that could've been summed up by saying there's only so much coaching can do. "I always tell the coaches, it's 50% on us. It's got to transfer — the message has to get through."

This message, and many others about how they want to play basketball on both ends, is getting through — just not to the guys who will ultimately determine wins and losses in the spring.

The Cavs won the minutes Omier played by 11. Getting quality play like this from a two-way big went a long way toward stealing the victory.

Irving received a loud ovation when he was introduced as a starter, and an even louder one when they played a video tribute for his contribution to the 2016 championship team.

"You know, 52 years is a long, long period of time waiting for a championship, and we did it. And I think that's one of the hardest championships — I think. I'm not gonna say it is, I said I think personally it's one of the hardest championships ever in NBA history that we got to accomplish."

The Cavs grabbed 57% of available rebounds (92nd percentile).

The Cavaliers who were available on Saturday showed that they're capable of hearing the message from the coaching staff and applying it on the court.

I wrote about this in yesterday's deep dive from the Miami Heat loss: The Cavs have quietly turned over their supporting cast. Their younger bench players are far more versatile than the specialists we've seen in previous seasons.

The trio of Tyrese Proctor, Meleek Thomas, and Jaylon Tyson have very defined roles when the starters are out there. They've mostly performed well in those situations and have shown glimpses of being able to do more. On Saturday, they proved that they can scale their games up when called upon.

Cleveland needed that trio to be the offensive initiators given who was out, and they did that. The ball was moving quickly around the perimeter, with each being able to attack off the dribble when needed. This resulted in the trio all scoring 18 points or more against a Mavs team actually playing their starters.

This performance on its own doesn't necessarily mean much. It's not uncommon to see NBA teams let up and lose when their opponent is missing half of their rotation. However, this game didn't just come out of the blue; it was a continuation of what we've been seeing pretty consistently for the last week or so — specifically from Proctor and Thomas.

The Cavs are at a weird crossroads. How they've been playing with their star players has led to inconsistent results. We've seen some high highs, but some even lower lows. Something needs to change if they're going to reach the heights they have the potential to.

Surrendering the reins to the young players isn't the answer to their problems. But turning to the style of play that this group has embraced — and the one that all the Cavs did during the 2024-25 season — could be a solution. Just not one we've seen the veterans truly get on board with yet. Maybe this win changes some minds.

#2770. Packers-Vikings Final Score: Green Bay’s comedy of errors continues

The Green Bay Packers' 30-10 loss to the Minnesota Vikings on Sunday had a little bit of everything: the offensive line getting their butt kicked, the rest of the offense having crucial errors, special teams woes and a small window of hope where it looked like the team was going to piece it together — just to let down the fans at Lambeau Field again.

Pre-game, it was revealed that not only was guard Kevin Zeitler, who played pretty solid last week against the New England Patriots, not going to start, but he wasn't going to play at all today. Zeitler, who came into the weekend listed as questionable with ankle and triceps injuries, was inactive.

Instead, the offensive line was comprised of Jordan Morgan, Laken Tomlinson, John Williams, Travis Glover and Anthony Belton (left to right), while Aaron Banks, previously part of the three-man guard rotation last week, watched from the sideline. That offensive line didn't give the Packers the shot in the arm that the Packers probably expected, as the team only ran for 56 yards on Sunday and surrendered an astonishing eight sacks.

From the jump, this game was off the rails for the offense.

On the opening drive for head coach Matt LaFleur's offense, tight end Mark Redman fumbled the ball. The defense held the Vikings to -2 yards over the first two drives, but Minnesota came away with three points on the board due to their starting field position.

Then rookie kicker Trey Smack, who came into the week as the second most costly kicker in the league this year behind only Harrison Butker, missed a field goal when Green Bay was finally in scoring position. After that, the Packers declined a penalty to attempt to push the Vikings out of field goal range on their next drive, allowing Minnesota to not only to convert that third down but also score a touchdown to go up 10-0.

Jordan Love later threw an interception in the red zone, where it was unclear whether or not tight end Tucker Kraft was expecting the football, which halted the offense's momentum.

The next score again came from Minnesota, which converted a long third down when the Packers played dime defense — a package the team rarely uses. The second slot in these looks is usually cornerback Carrington Valentine, who had to play right cornerback for the injured Brandon Cisse on this play — forcing Kamal Hadden into this role. That conversion set up a touchdown for former Packers running back Aaron Jones to punch in a score to go up 17-0, and in the stands at Lambeau Field for what might be his final leap in the stadium.

Xavier McKinney intercepted Kirk Cousins late in the first half, but the Packers weren't able to do anything with the possession because they took multiple sacks on the drive. Coming out of halftime, the offense posted just 57 yards and was averaging 2.2 yards per play.

The first play of the second half was fitting: Yet another sack. A bad snap from third-string center John Williams led to the Vikings recovering the ball just outside of the red zone. The Packers' defense held Minnesota to a field goal, making it 20-0.

This is when momentum started to change for Green Bay. After playing multiple tight end sets for the majority of the game (they averaged 1.8 tight ends played in the first half), LaFleur began to spread out the offense with three-receiver looks regularly. Undrafted rookie receiver J. Michael Sturdivant, who played just one snap in the first half, caught the first touchdown of his NFL career to put the Packers on the board 20-7. Shortly after, another field goal cut the lead to 20-10.

From there on out, it was a game against the clock. Minnesota realized this, draining the clock starting in the mid-third quarter. The Vikings had a long sustained drive that took nearly seven minutes of time off the clock in just 11 plays.

They were able to finish it off with a touchdown when linebacker Isaiah McDuffie dropped out with an injury. For one play, and one play only, Nick Niemann, a core special teamer, had to play the Mike position in place of McDuffie. On that one play, Kevin O'Connell's offense cooked up all sorts of eye candy to isolate Niemann on a tight end, who ended up in the end zone to put Minnesota up 27-10.

Before the Packers' offense got the ball again, Sturdivant, the spark that started Green Bay's offense earlier in the game, fumbled on a kickoff and the ball was recovered by the Vikings. Sturdivant was put into the game on kickoff returns because two other receivers-returners, Bo Melton and Skyy Moore, left the game with injuries.

Minnesota kicked another field goal in garbage time, but the game ended on the Sturdivant fumble. The whole game, outside of a two-drive stretch on offense, it looked like this team wanted to be put out of their misery.

Four turnovers. Eight sacks. 56 yards rushing. Special teams still not being fixed. Reserves on defense getting picked on because they're forced into the game due to injuries. Your 2026 Green Bay Packers!

#2768. Fading Friday night lights: Massachusetts tackles declining high school football participation

For some high schools in Massachusetts, Friday night lights are fading.

"The NFL has never been more popular, but high school [football] in Massachusetts, you can't argue with those numbers," Erik Ormberg, Medfield head football coach, said. "It is a concern."

The latest high school participation numbers present a troubling trend. The National Federation of High School Associations released its athletics participation statistics for the 2025-26 school year.

The data shows football is still the most popular high school sports nationally and in-state, but in Massachusetts, participation numbers have declined 18% over the last decade.

Boston 25 sports reporter Lauren Walsh spoke to multiple high school football coaches and administrators to dig into why this is happening.

"There are so many distractions, and you can easily play NCAA college football or pro football on your phone or your computer," Catholic Memorial head coach John DiBiaso said. "Why come out and sweat and work hard and sacrifice when you can take an easy route out?"

"Maybe families are also looking at the safety concerns," Millis athletic director Mike Rector suggested. "I know there have been talks, not just at Millis, but at other schools, where some families are nervous to have their child play football, which I don't blame at all."

"I think Covid had something to do with it," Ormberg said. "I think the declining population in the state of Massachusetts has something to do with it. I think in this internet age, there is some scary stuff out there."

The low and declining high school football participation numbers are evident from large to small schools.

"We had 75 kids ten years ago and now we have 41 guys that are playing nine through twelfth grade," Ormberg said.

Millis athletic director Mike Rector shared their football program only has 25 kids, 17 of which are graduating seniors.

Millis was one of five high schools that had to forfeit games in the month of October. The others were Manchester Essex, Lexington, Saugus, and Quabbin Regional. The reason for the forfeits was consistent among those schools.

"That came to the number of injuries, unfortunately," Rector said. "We had some injuries before the season, and then when we were around that week, we realized that we had roughly around 15 kids, and that just wasn't safe enough for us to play."

So it turns out, the country's most popular high school sport is starting to need a sales pitch for participation.

"I am constantly trying to recruit kids and families," Ormberg said. "I don't recruit players; I recruit moms. I think we have to build the trust with the parents and hopefully you'll see that during the tackling drills."

Medfield head coach Erik Ormberg does a tackling clinic as part of his practices, twice a week, to try to reduce injury and increase confidence in executing a safe and effective tackle.

"As a football coach, as a parent, as a school counselor, I want kids to be able to do dangerous things and hard things as safe as they can," Ormberg shared. "They get behind the wheel of a car, that's a dangerous thing. We are trying to do everything we can to make a risky sport as safe as possible."

Rosters around the state might be shrinking, but the love for the game is still growing in Massachusetts.

"Ten years ago, there were Super Bowls won here and the community has been so great and we're hoping to continue to build that because it's not just for Millis High School, it's also for the community itself," Rector said.

"For some of them, these two and a half hours pushing that sled is the best part of their day, and I would hate to see something like that go away," Ormberg said. "For the kids that are passionate about competing on Friday night, there is something special about those Friday night lights, and I hope they stick around long after my coaching days are over."

#2767. The case for a Domantas Sabonis trade for Utah

The Utah Jazz are in the midst of the worst start in decades. It's an even worse start than the former tanking seasons. Before the season started, the Jazz said they would have a competitive season. And even though the Jazz are close in a lot of these games, they are 1-12 and on an 11-game losing streak. With the Walker Kessler trade behind them, the Jazz are left with a lopsided team in desperate need of starting center play that could support their three young stars: Darryn Peterson, Ace Bailey, and Keyonte George.

That group this season has been elite, hiding behind subpar play from their center and bench. From CraftedNBA.com, here are the Jazz's best players sorted by net rating and showing their best On-Off players.

Utah's trio of Bailey, Peterson, and George is playing winning basketball, and it's an incredible sign for Utah. But outside of those three, it's not looking good. Losing Jaren Jackson Jr. at the beginning of the season has been brutal because his two-way play would have been a huge part of helping Utah get some wins.

Probably the biggest disappointment, though, is Lauri Markkanen's play this season. His off-ball offensive game is high-level as usual, the problem is everything else. He's not producing on the ball, and he has not been a good defender. That's why Utah should consider a potential move for Domantas Sabonis, who wants out of Sacramento. The trade is an easy, player-for-player trade with Lauri Markkanen.

I've thought about this a lot this season, and the more I think about it, the more I like it. Yes, Sabonis has some flaws just like Markkanen does. He's undersized at his position and not a good rim protector. He also struggles in space and on the perimeter defensively, just like Markkanen.

But Sabonis' pros would be huge for this Jazz team and their young COR3, and could provide a skill set that would take them to another level. Yes, Markkanen provides catch-and-shoot threes and cuts to the basket. Utah is getting enough shooting, scoring, and cutting from that young trio. What Utah lacks right now is a starting center. Sabonis would bring what they've been dying for all season, including elite rebounding, screening, passing, and inside scoring. Adding Sabonis would solidify the position in a way that they haven't had all season. Yes, the Jazz can simply wait for Jusuf Nurkic to come back, but with Nurkic's size and his foot injury, it's not a given that he'll stay healthy this season. Also, having Nurkic come back and play 20-25 minutes a night off the bench is best anyway.

Sabonis is also a perfect fit with what Will Hardy does on offense. He can get the ball and run a dribble handoff from the three-point line, then dive, or run simple pick-and-rolls as the screener. With Sabonis setting screens and scoring inside, you can see how Utah's offense could get to an elite level. It's also interesting to think about how well Ace Bailey would fit playing off of him. Sabonis is no Jokic, but he's the next best thing at center, hitting cutters and passing out of the short roll to open shooters. And finally, Sabonis can score inside on a mismatch. If Utah has a mismatch down low, Sabonis can easily take advantage. And when Jaren Jackson Jr. comes back, Sabonis is the perfect fit next to him, with their strengths offsetting each other. Jackson can be the weak-side defender while Sabonis cleans up their rebounding issues.

There's also an elephant in the room for the Jazz right now that needs to be recognized. Lauri Markkanen has simply not lived up to his superstar-level contract. With a move like this, Utah can get off his contract before it becomes untradable. Sabonis' contract is for this season and next, so if we're simply looking at contract relief, it's another bonus for Utah. If Markkanen is also a consistent injury risk and not an elite on-ball scorer over the next three seasons, his contract becomes an albatross with two years after this season and the final year coming in at $53M. Utah can get off that contract sooner rather than later by making this happen. Sabonis' contract runs through this season and next, and then Utah can either extend him or look for another option that fits the timeline of Keyonte George, Darryn Peterson, and Ace Bailey.

For Sacramento, Lauri Markkanen is the perfect fit for Darius Acuff Jr. Acuff can be the hub of everything they do, and Markkanen can do what he does best next to him: take catch-and-shoot threes and score off the ball. Markkanen also provides more spacing for Acuff, which can help him take his game to another level.

Honestly, this one makes a ton of sense for both sides. Most likely, no picks trade sides in this, and Utah can then use their picks from the Kessler trade in other ways, or just simply draft a center prospect for the post-Sabonis era.

The Vikings defense did what the Vikings defense does, but they also got some help from their offense and special teams on Sunday.

Minnesota torched Green Bay 30-10, winning in Kirk Cousins' first game back in a Vikings uniform. It is the most points the Vikings have scored since a 39-22 shellacking of the Packers in Week 1.

The Vikings ended a two-game losing streak and moved to 7-2, while the Packers dropped to 6-4.

#2764. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

#2762. Aidan Hutchinson got hurt celebrating a sack. A Lions LB thought he was joking

Detroit Lions edge rusher Aidan Hutchinson suffered a serious-looking knee injury in his team's 35-10 win over the New England Patriots in Munich.

Hutchinson's injury occurred as he celebrated a sack of Patriots quarterback Drake Maye in the first quarter of the Week 10 game. The 26-year-old jumped in the air and as he landed, his right knee buckled. He collapsed to the turf in pain as a result of that.

While Hutchinson immediately knew something was wrong, his plight didn't immediately register with his teammates, as Lions linebacker Derrick Barnes explained following the game.

"You know how people do the fake injury thing and they get up and start doing the dance?" Barnes told the Detroit Free Press, part of the USA TODAY Network, in the locker room after the game. "Aidan being as goofy as he is, I thought that's what he was doing."

"And then I looked, I was like, 'Aidan,' I was like, 'Don't scare me like that,' cause I thought he was just joking," Barnes continued. "And he was just like, 'I'm not.' I said, 'Are you serious?' He's like, 'Yeah.'"

Lions coach Dan Campbell told reporters during a postgame news conference the team did not have any further updates on Hutchinson's status. The star pass rusher will undergo further evaluation of his injury once the team returns to the United States.

That said, multiple reports indicate that Hutchinson's injury is "feared" to be significant. So, certainly, his injury is no joke, and the Lions may need to prepare to be without the two-time Pro Bowler for the long haul.

#2761. Barella: ‘Italy have more regret on first Belgium game, must keep tempo up’

Nicolò Barella insists Italy feel 'more regret' over the first Nations League defeat to Belgium than this 4-1 loss, but they 'cannot play at a low tempo, or we concede a lot of goals.'

This Nations League campaign has been a real rollercoaster for the Azzurri, who started with a 2-0 home defeat to Belgium on September 25 that made some talk about the possibility of relegation to League B.

BRUSSELS, BELGIUM – NOVEMBER 15: Pio Esposito of Italy interacts with Roberto Mancini, Head Coach of Italy, after the UEFA Nations League 2026/27 group phase match between Belgium and Italy at King Baudouin Stadium on November 15, 2026 in Brussels, Belgium. (Photo by Dean Mouhtaropoulos/Getty Images)

By the time they rolled around to Brussels seven weeks later, they were top of the group after a 1-1 draw in Paris and 3-1 victory over France at San Siro.

It is therefore a blow to end it with a 4-1 defeat that knocks them down to third place, especially as Pio Esposito had opened the scoring and later saw a penalty parried, around the goals from Maxim De Cuyper, Nathan Ngoy, Jeremy Doku and Milan winger Diego Moreira.

BRUSSELS, BELGIUM – NOVEMBER 15: Nathan Ngoy of Belgium shoots during the UEFA Nations League 2026/27 group phase match between Belgium and Italy at King Baudouin Stadium on November 15, 2026 in Brussels, Belgium. (Photo by Dean Mouhtaropoulos/Getty Images)

"Belgium were better than us tonight, while we also felt some fatigue from the last game with France," Barella told RAI Sport.

"We have shown in this Nations League campaign that we are here, and while we certainly need to improve, there are some solid foundations to build on."

What could explain the difference in performance from Thursday at San Siro to this evening in Brussels?

"We are a team that cannot play at a low tempo, if we do, then we concede a lot of goals. Quite a few of them were scored with ricochets and scrambles, so without taking credit away from Belgium, we also need to be more alert and not allow those situations to develop in the first place."

Nicolò Barella during an Italy training session (@azzurri)

There are positives to be seen, because considering how badly Italy lost the first match at the Stadio Olimpico just seven weeks ago, it was a remarkable turnaround to go into the final game as group leaders.

"We honestly have more regrets from the first match in Rome, whereas today Belgium were just better, we have no complaints," confessed Barella.

"That game was the start of our journey, but since then we've shown that we are ready and want to follow the ideas of this coach. The new lads are also bringing fresh energy to the squad.

"The defeat hurts, of course, but it mustn't make us lose sight of the main focus. We've got to get enthusiasm back into the fans, that is the most important thing at this moment."

Brooklyn Nets head coach Jordi Fernandez gestures from the sideline in the second half of an NBA basketball game against the Memphis Grizzlies in Memphis, Tenn. AP Photo/Brandon Dill

"Yeah, obviously it was something that we couldn't control," Fernández said. "And to be honest with you, it was not that bad. #2756. Falcons – Chiefs instant recap: It’s over

Even in lost seasons, you wait for the Falcons to make it official. Atlanta had shown enough life in the recent past to think they might have a surprise or two left in them at 3-6, and so while I counseled that you should leave your expectations behind for the rest of 2026, I did indulge in a little hope. No longer.

This season is over. The 3-7 Falcons, who can do nothing on offense despite the presence of a top ten selection at quarterback in Michael Penix Jr., maybe the best running back in football in Bijan Robinson, a great receiver in Drake London, and a well-paid tight end room and offensive line, are cooked. Their defense is far more game and has the foundation of a really good unit, but as the afternoon wore on, they devolved into frustration and sloppiness. This is a team that is not ready to be great, and aside from a couple of beautiful weeks, they don't even look ready to be competent.

We know how this goes. The coaching staff is not getting fired after one year, the front office is just beginning to piece this team together, and there's still seven games to go. The Falcons must play out the string and try to figure out what they want to be, and it will be up to this still-fresh regime to make 2026 and the years preceding it a distant memory. We have put our hope in that outcome over and over again and have gotten nothing from it, so we are bucking history when we assume things will be different in 2027. What choice do we have, though?

#2755. Nets can’t overcome slow start in 129-110 loss to Grizzlies

Those are words you never want to hear from the head coach of a rebuilding team. Especially not in Year 3.

The Brooklyn Nets had a chance to build on their blowout win over New Orleans. Mikel Brown Jr. was back after missing the last two games. There was a small window to finally pick up some momentum. Instead, they followed it up with another frustrating loss, 129-110, in the finale of a four-game road-trip.

Brooklyn fell behind early and trailed 38-23 after the first quarter. You'd never think Memphis played a game just the night before.

The Nets struggled to contain Memphis from the perimeter and committed 16 turnovers that led to 30 Grizzlies points. They made a push in the third quarter and cut a 23-point deficit to eight, but couldn't get the stops necessary to make it a game.

"I think we cut it to seven at some point and you should have been in the game, but we are not mature enough… we played against a team that played in a second of a back-to-back with the same travel that we had, and they played the right way, played really hard and we're not there yet. So, you know, go back and work and find ways to get better, because when you're not consistent and you don't find those ways, it's just like that's not what we want to do here," Fernandez said after the game.

The Nets briefly led 7-5, but Memphis responded with a 9-0 run and never let up control. Cedric Coward, Cam Spencer and Jaylen Wells got hot early, and a Wells' three-pointer forced a Brooklyn timeout with 7:55 remaining in the first quarter, with the Grizzlies flipping the score to 16-9 in their own favor.

The shooting didn't slow down. Memphis made nine three-pointers in the opening quarter and carried a 38-23 lead into the second. Brooklyn faced a 19-point deficit in the first half before making a small dent in it. The Grizzlies still led 71-54 at halftime.

"Right now the two practices we've had, we've practiced very well. So we practice better than we play, which is not a terrible sign. It's a frustrating sign because you believe you're better, but all we can do is go back to work."

Julius Randle said Brooklyn didn't match Memphis' urgency from the opening tip. The three-time All-Star pointed to the team's overall start as a bigger issue.

"We didn't punch first. We didn't come out with a sense of urgency, and they took advantage of that," he said.

"Just organization. I think maybe just trying too hard, playing a little unorganized, stuff like that," Randle said. "And then just attention to detail, you know, just think we got a lapse of focus sometimes."

Sparked by scoring from Noah Clowney, Day'Ron Sharpe, and Julius Randle, Brooklyn put together a strong third-quarter surge to cut the deficit to 85-77 with 5:12 remaining. Memphis took a 103-84 lead into the fourth quarter and eventually stretched the margin beyond 20.

It was Brooklyn's fifth loss by double figures.

"They got to grow fast," Fernandez said about the young group. "You know, like being 20 is not an excuse.…. I'm frustrated or I get mad because I know they're very good and they're better than this. And when I just don't see a connected group is, it hurts…"

Michael Porter Jr. attended Saturday's game after missing the game for personal reasons. Fernández was asked about Porter's absence and whether he knew when the forward might return, but the coach declined.

Mikel Brown Jr. returned after missing the last two games. It's been a bit of a strange start and the guard missed the last two games for two different reasons — one for injury management and the other for an illness. The rookie finished with six points and two assists on 2-of-5 shooting in 20 minutes.

His father followed with some sound advice.

Brooklyn's trip to Memphis didn't go according to plan. The Nets were caught in Friday night's FAA ground stop after boarding their charter flight out of New Orleans, forcing the team to stay overnight before finally flying out Saturday morning.

The team arrived in Memphis around 11 a.m., just hours before its game against the Grizzlies. The unexpected delay also forced Brooklyn to scrap its planned morning shootaround.

"Yeah, I mean, obviously it was something that we couldn't control," head coach Jordi Fernández said. "And to be honest with you, it was not that bad. We had good rest and got here [Saturday] morning and did our walkthrough, and probably [would have] gone to the gym, but we were not able to. And I think we feel good and ready to go."

Fernández acknowledged that the altered schedule wasn't ideal, particularly for players who had grown accustomed to the team's usual pregame routine.

"Everybody's safe. It's the most important thing," he said.

The Nets are back home against the 76ers on Monday, November 16.

#2754. Juice your growth and save on taxes in this workplace investment account

Workers are in the heart of open enrollment season for next year's benefits, and rising healthcare costs are shining a spotlight on an offering that includes a tax-advantaged investment account.

Healthcare expenses are climbing for workers and employers alike, with average total family premiums, including the employer's share, averaging $28,303, up 5% from a year ago, according to data from health policy organization KFF, formerly the Kaiser Family Foundation.

To manage the costs, a growing number of employers are moving to high-deductible health plans, which work alongside health savings accounts or HSAs. Almost 40% of companies with at least 10 employees that offer health benefits also have an HSA-qualified plan, KFF found.

Health savings accounts have three major tax advantages. First, contributions that your employer automatically deducts from your pay aren't taxed. Second, money stashed into the HSA grows free of taxes. Finally, you can make tax-free withdrawals for qualified health expenses – just be sure to save your receipts.

"I love HSAs because of their triple-tax advantage," said Eileen Olson, CFP and founder of Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina, near Savannah, Georgia. "For people who can afford to leave it alone, it can be one of the best retirement accounts they're going to have."

In 2027, those with individual healthcare coverage through a high-deductible plan can stash up to $4,500 in an HSA, including any contributions from employers. That number goes up to $9,000 for family plans. Individuals can kick in a catch-up contribution of $1,000 if they're aged 55 and older and not enrolled in Medicare.

The HSA differs from the healthcare flexible spending account, known as the FSA.

FSA funds must be used by the end of the plan year unless your employer allows a carryover of up to $700 or an additional 2.5 months to use the money. HSAs, however, have no expiration date on their funds – and that's what makes them such a powerful tool for long-term planning. You can also take the HSA with you if you change employers.

"Get the money deployed, take advantage of the pretax contribution, and allocate the money into a growth portfolio," said Mark Stancato, certified financial planner at VIP Wealth Advisors in Decatur, Georgia. "With the HSA, you can keep compounding year over year."

The structure of an HSA and the available investment menu will vary from one plan to the next, and some will require that employees keep some of their holdings in cash.

Preset HSA fund menus tend to have 15 to 25 funds to choose from, and providers say that accountholders who invest tend to use two to four funds, according to Greg Carlson, senior research analyst for equity strategies at Morningstar.

How you allocate your HSA will depend on several factors, including your family's health prospects in the next year, your cash flow needs – how much you can comfortably sock into the HSA – and your risk tolerance, said André Small, CFP and founder of A Small Investment in Houston.

Small's standard is to keep an amount equal to 120% of the plan's out-of-pocket maximum held in cash in the HSA.

"That covers a bad year without forcing a sale at the wrong time," he said. "Above that amount? I typically advise investing the amount the client is comfortable investing, allocated to their risk tolerance, their time horizon and the goal of spending these dollars on care later."

For 2027, the IRS defines a high-deductible plan as one with an annual deductible no less than $1,750 for self-only coverage ($3,500 for family plans) and with an out-of-pocket expense limit that doesn't exceed $8,700 for self-only (or $17,400 for family coverage). Out-of-pocket expenses include deductibles, copays and other costs – but not premiums.

Other advisors suggest having up to the deductible in cash, though some HSA plan providers require a stated minimum amount in cash.

Beyond this cash reserve, financial advisors recommend allocating into stocks.

Patrick Swift, CFP and president of Amplius Wealth Advisors in Blue Bell, Pennsylvania, aims for an allocation of up to 60% in U.S. large cap stocks, 20% in small and mid-cap stocks and 20% in international stocks.

"We believe the equity portfolio should be well diversified," he said. "If the [HSA] balance is 2% to 10% of your net worth, why not be aggressive? That can change based on the situation, but if you are comfortable with the philosophical aspect, why not be growth oriented?"

Similarly, Stancato likes to go heavy on equity exposure in HSA accounts. "I allocate more dollars into small caps and emerging markets – those asset classes with more volatility but also more opportunity for growth," the advisor said. He also adds some large-cap domestic exposure.

For the adventurous, select HSA providers are offering brokerage window access, meaning investors can pick from a broader array of investments, including individual stocks.

This year, seven providers surveyed by Morningstar offer access to brokerage windows – up from four in 2023 – including HealthEquity, Fidelity and Lively. Be aware that there may be annual fees attached, either a flat dollar amount or a percentage of assets.

Rachel Elson, a CFP and wealth advisor at Perigon Wealth Management in San Francisco, recommends keeping things simple. "You can do a three-ETF model, covering some allocation that includes the U.S. market, and two ETFs for developed and emerging markets."

"You don't need to get super complicated here," she added.

#2753. Bills rout Jets 31-12, improve to 5-0 on the road this season

The Bills are hoping to earn home field advantage in the AFC playoffs and their success on the road could make that happen.

Josh Allen ran for two touchdowns and James Cook scored for the eighth straight game to power Buffalo to a 31-12 win over the Jets at MetLife Stadium. The win has them 5-0 on the road this season and they are 8-1 overall on the season.

Thanks to the Patriots’ loss in Germany, the Bills now have a comfortable lead in the AFC East and their attention will likely be focused on making sure they wind up as the top seed in the conference.

Allen is now up to 11 rushing touchdowns on the season and he’s tied with Eric Dickerson and Curtis Martin for 14th all-time with 90 career rushing scores. He also threw a touchdown to help the winning effort.

Cook’s touchdown was a 74-yard sprint for the final points of the game in the fourth quarter. That touchdown came after the Jets lost a fumble while trying a lateral after a catch by wide receiver Garrett Wilson on a pass by Cade Klubnik. The rookie saw his first action of the regular season because Geno Smith wound up getting banged up at multiple points.
